<div class="display-inline-block"><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer text-no-underline bg-transparent border-0 padding-0 text-left margin-0 text-normal text-primary" aria-controls="journal_context_menu">Comput Struct Biotechnol J</button></div>. 2023 Jul 29;21:3796–3809. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.csbj.2023.07.037" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1016/j.csbj.2023.07.037</a>

<hgroup><h1>Characterizations of annexin A1-interacting proteins in apical membrane and cytosolic compartments of renal tubular epithelial cells</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Peerapen%20P"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Paleerath Peerapen</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Paleerath Peerapen</span></h3>
<div class="p">

<sup>1</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Boonmark%20W"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Wanida Boonmark</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">
<h3><span class="name western">Wanida Boonmark</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Medical Proteomics Unit, Research Department, Faculty of Medicine Siriraj Hospital, Mahidol University, Bangkok 10700, Thailand</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Boonmark%20W"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Wanida Boonmark</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Thongboonkerd%20V"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id3"><span class="name western">Visith Thongboonkerd</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id3">
<h3><span class="name western">Visith Thongboonkerd</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Medical Proteomics Unit, Research Department, Faculty of Medicine Siriraj Hospital, Mahidol University, Bangkok 10700, Thailand</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Thongboonkerd%20V"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Visith Thongboonkerd</span></a>
</div>

<sup>1,</sup><sup>⁎</sup>

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2023 Apr 22; Revised 2023 Jul 27; Accepted 2023 Jul 27; Collection date 2023.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2023 The Authors</div>
<p>This is an open access article under the CC BY-NC-ND license (http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC10407547  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37560129/" class="usa-link">37560129</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ab0010"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Annexin A1 (ANXA1) is a multifunctional calcium-binding protein that can bind to membrane phospholipids. Under high-calcium condition, ANXA1 expression increases on renal epithelial cell surface, leading to enhanced adhesion of calcium oxalate (CaOx) crystal (stone material) onto the cells. To regulate various cellular processes, ANXA1 interacts with many other intracellular protein partners. However, components of the ANXA1-interacting protein complex remain unclear. Herein, we characterized the interacting complexes of apical membrane (ApANXA1) and cytosolic (cyANXA1) forms of ANXA1 in apical membrane and cytosolic compartments, respectively, of renal epithelial cells under high-calcium condition using proteomic and bioinformatic approaches. After fractionation, the ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners were identified by immunoprecipitation followed by nanoLC‑ESI‑Qq-TOF tandem mass spectrometry (IP-MS/MS). The ANXA1-interacting partners that were common in both apical membrane and cytosolic compartments and those unique in each compartment were then analyzed for their physico-chemical properties (molecular weight, isoelectric point, amino acid contents, instability index, aliphatic index, and grand average of hydropathicity), secondary structure (α-helix, β-turn, random coil, and extended strand), molecular functions, biological processes, reactome pathways and KEGG pathways. The data demonstrated that each set of these interacting proteins exhibited common and unique characteristics and properties. The knowledge from this study may lead to better understanding of the ApANXA1 and CyAXNA1 biochemistry and functions as well as the pathophysiology of CaOx kidney stone formation induced by high-calcium condition.</p>
<section id="keys0005" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Bioinformatics, Calcium, Immunoprecipitation, Kidney stone, Mass spectrometry, Oxalate, Proteomics</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="ab0015"><h2>Graphical Abstract</h2>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0045" title="ga1"><p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_ga1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/95a2d372af47/ga1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="200" width="737" alt="ga1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0045/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section class="abstract" id="ab0020"><h2>Highlights</h2>
<ul id="li0005" class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li id="u0005">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0005">56 ApANXA1- and 69 CyANXA1-interacting partners were identified.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0010">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0010">37 were common but 19 and 32 were unique for ApANXA1 and CyANXA1, respectively.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0015">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0015">Various physico-chemical properties and other characteristics were analyzed.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0020">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0020">Each set of the interacting proteins had both common and unique characteristics.</p>
</li>
</ul></section><section id="sec0005"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p id="p0025">Annexin A1 (ANXA1) is a member of the annexins protein family with binding ability to calcium and membrane phospholipids, and plays several roles in cellular processes, including apoptosis, differentiation and proliferation <a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">[1]</a>. ANXA1 comprises four repeating motifs with calcium-binding domains <a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2">[2]</a>. This protein locates mainly in cytoplasm and is also associated with plasma membrane <a href="#bib3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib3">[3]</a>. Several studies have reported anti-inflammatory activity of ANXA1 <a href="#bib4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib4">[4]</a>, <a href="#bib5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib5">[5]</a>, <a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6">[6]</a>, and overexpression of ANXA1 in diabetic mice can relieve diabetes-induced kidney injury <a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7">[7]</a>. However, its overexpression is associated with several cancers <a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8">[8]</a>, <a href="#bib9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib9">[9]</a>, <a href="#bib10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib10">[10]</a> and antineutrophil cytoplasmic autoantibody-associated vasculitis <a href="#bib11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib11">[11]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0030">Kidney stone disease has been recognized as a global health problem that leads to significant economic burden <a href="#bib12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib12">[12]</a>, <a href="#bib13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib13">[13]</a>, <a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">[14]</a>. Beside the high prevalence and incidence, its high recurrence rate is one of the major problems for this disease <a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15">[15]</a>. To prevent this disease more effectively and to reduce its economic and other burdens, clearer understanding of its etiologic mechanisms is required. Crystal retention onto renal cell surface is one of the critical processes during initial phase for development of kidney stones, especially calcium oxalate (CaOx), which is the most predominant stone type <a href="#bib16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib16">[16]</a>, <a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17">[17]</a>. CaOx crystal retention on the cell surface is affected by several factors, including cellular injury <a href="#bib18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib18">[18]</a>, <a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19">[19]</a>, <a href="#bib20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib20">[20]</a> and abundance of crystal receptors <a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">[21]</a>, <a href="#bib22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib22">[22]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0035">Hypercalciuria is known as one of the risk factors for CaOx kidney stone disease <a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23">[23]</a>, <a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24">[24]</a>. Several lines of evidence have reported that high urinary calcium concentration is associated with renal cell injury <a href="#bib25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib25">[25]</a>, <a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">[26]</a>, <a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27">[27]</a>. High-calcium condition also increases CaOx crystal retention onto renal epithelial cell surface <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>, <a href="#bib29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib29">[29]</a> accompanied with the increased expression of surface ANXA1 <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>, which is one of the CaOx crystal receptors <a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">[30]</a>, <a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31">[31]</a>, <a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32">[32]</a>. A previous study using conventional and two-dimensional (2D) Western blot analyses has revealed that a high-molecular-weight form of ANXA1 (at approximately 100 kDa) is expressed on apical membrane, whereas another form with regular molecular weight (at approximately 37 kDa) is present in the cytosol of renal epithelial cells <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>. Additionally, the high-calcium condition increases expression levels of both apical membrane and cytosolic forms of ANXA1 in renal epithelial cells <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>. Moreover, the decreased expression of cell surface ANXA1 by caffeine or estrogen treatment can reduce CaOx crystal retention on renal epithelial cells <a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31">[31]</a>, <a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32">[32]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0040">Similar to other cellular proteins, ANXA1 is expected to interact with many other cellular proteins to govern cellular functions. However, components of the interacting partners of apical membrane ANXA1 (ApANXA1) and cytosolic ANXA1 (CyANXA1) remain unclear. We speculated that the ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners might have some different identities, properties and functions. The present study thus aimed to identify and characterize the ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners in apical membrane and cytosolic compartments, respectively, of renal epithelial cells under high-calcium condition (to increase expression of ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a> and to enhance identification of their interacting complexes). Their interacting complexes were immunoprecipitated and then characterization by tandem mass spectrometry and bioinformatic analyses.</p></section><section id="sec0010"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials &amp; methods</h2>
<section id="sec0015"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Cell cultivation and high-calcium treatment</h3>
<p id="p0045">MDCK renal cells (ATCC; Manassas, VA) were maintained in a complete medium containing DMEM (Gibco; Grand Island, NY) supplemented with 60 U/ml penicillin and 60 µg/ml streptomycin (Sigma-Aldrich; Saint Louis, MO) and 10% fetal bovine serum (Gibco) in an incubator with 5% CO<sub>2</sub> at 37 °C. The cells grown in 6-well plate (Corning Costar; Cambridge, MA) served as the non-polarized cells, whereas cell polarization was induced in a Transwell (0.4-µm pore size) (Corning Costar) as previously described <a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">[21]</a>, <a href="#bib33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib33">[33]</a>. The cells were then incubated with 20 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub> (Sigma-Aldrich) for 72 h prior to investigations as follows.</p></section><section id="sec0020"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Immunofluorescence staining and laser-scanning confocal microscopy</h3>
<p id="p0050">Immunofluorescence staining and laser-scanning confocal microscopy were performed as described previously <a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34">[34]</a>, <a href="#bib35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib35">[35]</a> to confirm that cell polarization was successful. Briefly, non-polarized and polarized MDCK cells were washed twice with ice-cold PBS containing 0.1 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub> and 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>. The cells were then fixed with 4% paraformaldehyde/PBS at 25 °C for 15 min and permeabilized with 0.1% Triton X-100/PBS at 25 °C for 15 min. After blocking followed by three washes with PBS, the cells were incubated with rabbit polyclonal anti-podocalyxin (gp135), rabbit polyclonal anti-calpain-1, or mouse monoclonal anti-Na<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup>-ATPase antibody (all were from Santa Cruz Biotechnology; Santa Cruz, CA, and diluted 1:50 in 1% BSA/PBS) at 37 °C for 1 h. After three washes with PBS, the cells were further incubated with corresponding secondary antibody conjugated with Alexa Fluor 488 (Invitrogen; Paisley, UK) (1:2000 in 1% BSA/PBS) mixed with Hoechst’s nuclear dye (Invitrogen) (0.1 µg/ml in 1% BSA/PBS) at 37 °C for 1 h. After three washes with PBS, the cells were mounted with 50% glycerol/PBS and examined under a laser-scanning confocal microscope (Eclipse Ti-Clsi4 Laser Unit) (Nikon; Tokyo, Japan) equipped with NIS-Elements AR software (Nikon).</p></section><section id="sec0025"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Fractionation of apical membrane and cytosolic proteins</h3>
<p id="p0055">Peeling method was performed to fractionate and purify apical membrane and cytosolic proteins as described previously <a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">[30]</a>, <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a>. After 72-h treatment with 20 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub>, the polarized cells were washed with ice-cold PBS containing 0.1 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub> and 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>. A filter paper (Whatman; Maidstone, UK) pre-wetted with deionized water was placed on top of the polarized cells for 5 min and then peeled off. Apical membranes adhered and retained under the filter paper were harvested by soaking the filter paper into deionized water followed by gentle scrapping. The remaining cell shafts were then collected as the cytosolic fraction. After drying by lyophilization, proteins in both apical membrane and cytosolic fractions were extracted by using modified RIPA buffer (0.5% Triton X-100, 1 mM EDTA, 150 mM NaCl and 50 mM Tris-HCl; pH 7.4). Concentrations of the recovered proteins were then measured using Bio-Rad Protein Assay (Bio-Rad Laboratories; Hercules, CA). Full details of the peeling procedures as well as the information to convince the efficacy and purity of this method to isolate/purify apical membrane proteins can be found in the previously published article by our group <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a>.</p></section><section id="sec0030"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Western blot analyses</h3>
<p id="p0060">Equally loaded proteins from each sample (30 µg/sample) were resolved by 12% SDS-PAGE, transferred onto nitrocellulose membrane (Whatman), and incubated with 5% skim milk in PBS for 1 h to avoid non-specific binding. After washing with PBS, the membrane was incubated with rabbit polyclonal anti-podocalyxin (gp135), rabbit polyclonal anti-calpain-1, or mouse monoclonal anti-ANXA1 primary antibody (all were from Santa Cruz Biotechnology and diluted 1:1000 in 1% skim milk/PBS) at 4 °C overnight. After washing with PBS, the membrane was incubated with corresponding secondary antibody conjugated with horseradish peroxidase (Sigma-Aldrich) (1:20,000 in 1% skim milk/PBS) at 25 °C for 1 h and washed with PBS. The membrane was then incubated with SuperSignal West Pico chemiluminescence substrate (Pierce Biotechnology, Inc.; Rockford, IL) to develop immunoreactive bands to be visualized on a radiographic film.</p></section><section id="sec0035"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Affinity purification by immunoprecipitation (IP)</h3>
<p id="p0065">IP was performed as described previously <a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">[37]</a>, <a href="#bib38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib38">[38]</a>. To remove non-specific affinity, proteins in apical membrane and cytosolic fractions were incubated with Protein G Sepharose beads (50% slurry) (GE Healthcare; Uppsala, Sweden) on a tube rotator at 4 °C for 15 min. After 5-min centrifugation at 1,500g, the supernatant was collected and incubated overnight with mouse monoclonal anti-ANXA1 antibody (Santa Cruz Biotechnology) or isotype-control mouse IgG (Santa Cruz Biotechnology) at 4 °C on the rotator. Protein G Sepharose beads (50% slurry) were then added and incubated with the sample on the rotator at 4 °C for 4 h. The beads were collected by 5-min centrifugation at 1,500g and washed with modified RIPA buffer five times. Finally, the protein complexes retained on the beads were eluted using Laemmli’s buffer.</p></section><section id="sec0040"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. In‑solution tryptic digestion and nanoLC‑ESI‑Qq-TOF MS/MS</h3>
<p id="p0070">In‑solution tryptic digestion and nanoLC‑ESI‑Qq-TOF MS/MS were done as previously described <a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">[39]</a>, <a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40">[40]</a>. Details are also provided in <a href="#sec0125" class="usa-link">Supplementary Methods</a>.</p></section><section id="sec0045"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Protein-protein interaction (PPI) network analysis</h3>
<p id="p0075">All sets of the identified proteins, including unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, and common ANXA1-interacting partners (proteins interacted with both ApANXA1 and CyANXA1), were submitted to STRING software (version 11.5) (<em>www.string-db.org</em>) to create their PPI networks.</p></section><section id="sec0050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.8. Analyses of physico-chemical properties</h3>
<p id="p0080">All sets of the identified proteins, including unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, and common ANXA1-interacting partners, were analyzed for various physico-chemical properties. Molecular weight and isoelectric point (pI) were retrieved from MASCOT search engine (<em>www.matrixscience.com</em>). Expasy ProtParam tool (<em>https://web.expasy.org/protparam</em>) was employed for computing other properties, such as percentage of amino acid contents, instability index, aliphatic index, and grand average of hydropathicity (GRAVY).</p></section><section id="sec0055"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.9. Secondary structure analysis</h3>
<p id="p0085">All sets of the identified proteins, including unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, and common ANXA1-interacting partners, were analyzed for their secondary structure components using the online Self-Optimized Prediction Method and Alignment (SOPMA) tool (<em>https://npsa-prabi.ibcp.fr/cgi-bin/npsa_automat.pl?page=/NPSA/npsa_sopma.html</em>). The default search parameters were used, including number of conformational states = 4 (helix, sheet, turn and coil), window width = 17, and similarity threshold = 8.</p></section><section id="sec0060"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.10. Functional enrichment analysis</h3>
<p id="p0090">All sets of the identified proteins, including unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, and common ANXA1-interacting partners, were analyzed for Gene Ontology (GO) molecular functions using the STRING software (version 11.5) (<em>www.string-db.org</em>). Additionally, the ClueGO plugin (version 2.5.9) (<em>https://apps.cytoscape.org/apps/cluego</em>) and the Cytoscape software (version 3.9.1) (<em>https://cytoscape.org</em>) were used to acquire the GO biological processes, reactome pathways and KEGG pathways of all these sets of these proteins.</p></section><section id="sec0065"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.11. Descriptive statistical analysis</h3>
<p id="p0095">Distribution of each physico-chemical property and percentage of each secondary structure component for the common ANXA1-, unique ApANXA1-, and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners are presented as box plots displaying five important values of each dataset, including minimum, maximum, median, first quartile and third quartile. The box plots were generated by using Microsoft Excel.</p></section></section><section id="sec0070"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<section id="sec0075"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Successful polarization of MDCK cells</h3>
<p id="p0100">To confirm that the cell polarization was successful, immunofluorescence staining was performed followed by laser-scanning confocal microscopy. In the non-polarized MDCK cells grown in 6-well plate, expression of markers for apical membrane (podocalyxin/gp135), cytoplasm (calpain-1) and basolateral membrane (Na<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup>-ATPase) was diffuse in the cell cytoplasm without membrane organization observed (<a href="#fig0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a><strong>A</strong>). In the polarized cells grown in the Transwell, podocalyxin/gp135, calpain-1 and Na<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup>-ATPase were specifically localized at apical membrane, cytoplasm and basolateral membrane, respectively (<a href="#fig0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a><strong>B</strong>). These data indicate that the cells were successfully polarized in the Transwell system.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0005" title="Fig. 1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/1a126eef1672/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="536" width="712" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: Successful polarization of MDCK cells.</strong> Immunofluorescence staining and laser-scanning confocal microscopy were performed to confirm that the cell polarization was successful. <strong>(A):</strong> Non-polarized MDCK cells cultured in the 6-well plate. <strong>(B):</strong> Polarized MDCK cells cultured in the Transwell system. All these cells were immuno-stained for podocalyxin (gp135), calpain-1, and Na<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup>-ATPase as the protein markers for apical membrane, cytoplasm, and basolateral membrane, respectively.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec0080"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Fractionation of apical membrane and cytosolic proteins and IP of the ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners</h3>
<p id="p0105">Apical membrane fraction was isolated by peeling method, whereas the remaining cell shafts served as the cytosolic fraction. To confirm purity of apical membrane and cytosolic proteins in corresponding fractions, Western blot analysis of apical membrane and cytosolic protein markers was performed. The data showed that podocalyxin (or gp135, which is an apical membrane protein marker) and calpain-1 (a cytosolic protein marker) were successfully enriched in apical membrane and cytosolic fractions, respectively, without cross-contaminations between these two fractions (<a href="#fig0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a><strong>A and B</strong>). We also confirmed that high-calcium condition (incubated with 20 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub>) successfully induced the upregulation of ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 as compared with the normal-calcium condition (<a href="#fig0010" class="usa-link">Figs. 2</a><strong>C and</strong>
<a href="#fig0010" class="usa-link">2</a><strong>D</strong>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0010" title="Fig. 2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/d8ef8da48ba3/gr2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1508" width="665" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: Fractionation of apical membrane and cytosolic proteins and upregulation of ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 by high-calcium. (A and B):</strong> Western blotting to demonstrate the successful enrichment of markers for apical membrane (podocalyxin or gp135) and cytosolic (calpain-1) proteins in apical membrane and cytosolic fractions, respectively, without cross-contaminations between these two fractions. <strong>(C and D):</strong> Western blotting to confirm that high-calcium condition (incubated with 20 mM CaCl<sub>2</sub>) successfully induced the upregulation of ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 in the apical membrane and cytosolic fractions, respectively. Note that the ApANXA1 was identified at high molecular sizes (∼ 100 kDa) in apical membrane fraction <strong>(C)</strong>, whereas CyANXA1 was identified at regular molecular size (∼ 37 kDa) in cytosolic fraction <strong>(D)</strong>. The arrow indicates the target protein.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0110">After affinity purification by IP, the efficacy of enrichment of ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 was evaluated. Western blotting revealed that ApANXA1 was successfully pulled down at high molecular sizes (≥ 100 kDa) from apical membrane fraction (<a href="#fig0015" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a><strong>A</strong>), whereas CyANXA1 was successfully pulled down at its regular molecular size (∼ 37 kDa) from cytosolic fraction (<a href="#fig0015" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a><strong>B</strong>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0015" title="Fig. 3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/5c76ff6ee18d/gr3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="608" width="748" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0015/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: IP of ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners. (A and B):</strong> Western blotting of ANXA1 after affinity purification by IP in apical membrane and cytosolic fractions, respectively. Note that the ApANXA1 was identified at high molecular sizes (≥ 100 kDa) in apical membrane fraction <strong>(A)</strong>, whereas CyANXA1 was identified at regular molecular size (∼ 37 kDa) in cytosolic fraction <strong>(B)</strong>. The arrow indicates the ANXA1 band.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec0085"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Identification of ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners by nanoLC‑ESI‑Qq-TOF MS/MS analysis</h3>
<p id="p0115">After IP, the ApANXA1- and CyANXA1-interacting partners were identified by nanoLC‑ESI‑Qq-TOF MS/MS analysis. Note that proteins, which were found in the isotype-control IP samples (non-specific binders), were excluded from the list. The results revealed a total of 56 ApANXA1-interacting partners (<a href="#tbl0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) and 69 CyANXA1-interacting partners (<a href="#tbl0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Among these, 37 proteins were identified in both sets and thus defined as the common ANXA1-interacting partners (labelled with ‡), whereas 19 proteins were exclusively identified as the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (labelled with *) and 32 proteins were exclusively identified as the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (labelled with #) (<a href="#tbl0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, <a href="#tbl0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a><strong>, and</strong>
<a href="#fig0020" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a><strong>A</strong>).</p>

</div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0020" title="Fig. 4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/7bb24ecbc082/gr4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1453" width="748" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0020/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: MS/MS identification and PPI network analysis of the ANXA1-interacting partners. (A):</strong> A Venn diagram summarizing numbers of the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (exclusively found in apical membrane compartment), common ANXA1-interacting partners (found in both cellular compartments), and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (exclusively found in cytosolic compartment) (see details in <a href="#tbl0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, <a href="#tbl0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). <strong>(B-D):</strong> PPI network analysis of the common ANXA1-, unique ApANXA1-, and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, respectively. The thickness of line representing each interaction indicates the confidence level.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec0090"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. PPI network analysis of the ANXA1-interacting partners</h3>
<p id="p0120">PPI networks of common ANXA1-, unique ApANXA1-, and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners were generated using the STRING tool. The results showed both direct and indirect interactions between ANXA1 and its interacting partners in each set of these proteins. Among the common ANXA1-interacting partners, AHSG, HSP90AA1, ANXA2, CFL1, YWHAX and HSPA8 were directly interacted with of ANXA1 (<a href="#fig0020" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a><strong>B</strong>). For the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, PRDX1, S100A11, CLU and EZR directly interacted with ANXA1 (<a href="#fig0020" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a><strong>C</strong>). Among the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, ENO1, PRDX2, HSPA4 and EEF2 had direct interactions with ANXA1 (<a href="#fig0020" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a><strong>D</strong>).</p></section><section id="sec0095"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Analyses of physico-chemical properties of the ANXA1-interacting partners</h3>
<p id="p0125">According to the data retrieved from the MASCOT search engine, range of molecular weights of the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (11.23–294.06 kDa) was greater than those of the common ANXA1- and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (9.75–85.12 kDa and 9.25–96.22 kDa, respectively) (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>A</strong>). The median for pI of the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (9.41) was greater than those of the common ANXA1- and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (7.62 and 7.74, respectively) (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>B</strong>). The Expasy ProtParam tool was utilized to analyze other physio-chemical properties of the ANXA1-interacting partners. Content analysis found that non-polar amino acids (Ala, Gly, Ile, Leu, Met, Pro and Val) were the major residues in the ANXA1-interacting partners in all sets with comparable medians of their percentages at 42.50%, 42.00%, and 42.45% for the common ANXA1-, unique ApANXA1-, and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, respectively (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>C</strong>). The medians of positively charged amino acids (Lys and Arg) were greater than those of negatively charged amino acids (Asp and Glu) in all three sets (12.80% vs. 11.50% for common ANXA1-interacting partners, 15.70% vs. 10.80% for unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, and 14.40% vs. 11.40% for unique CyANXA1-interacting partners) (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>C</strong>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0025" title="Fig. 5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/34992be8a4ee/gr5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1101" width="709" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0025/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: Analyses of physico-chemical properties of the ANXA1-interacting partners. (A):</strong> Box plot of molecular weight. <strong>(B):</strong> Box plot of pI. <strong>(C):</strong> Heatmap representing percentage of each type of amino acids. <strong>(D):</strong> Box plot of instability index. <strong>(E):</strong> Pie chart representing proportion of stable and unstable proteins based on instability index. <strong>(F):</strong> Box plot of aliphatic index. <strong>(G):</strong> Box plot of GRAVY value.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0130">The instability indices were at the ranges of 19.64–83.97, 25.20–58.66, and 6.72–73.48 for common ANXA1-, unique ApANXA1-, and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, respectively (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>D</strong>). Classification of stable and unstable proteins based on their instability indices (&lt;40 for the stable proteins) revealed that proportions of the stable proteins in the common ANXA1- and unique ApANXA1-interacting partners were greater than those of the unstable proteins (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>E</strong>). By contrast, the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners had greater proportion of the unstable proteins (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>E</strong>). Analysis of aliphatic index to indicate thermostability of the proteins found that median of aliphatic indices of the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners was lowest as compared with the common ANXA1- and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>F</strong>). Analysis of the grand average of hydropathicity (GRAVY) values revealed that most of the ANXA1-interacting partners in all sets were hydrophilic proteins (with negative GRAVY values) (<a href="#fig0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a><strong>G</strong>).</p></section><section id="sec0100"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Secondary structure analysis of the ANXA1-interacting partners</h3>
<p id="p0135">Percentages of four conformational states (α-helix, β-turn, random coil and extended strand/sheet) of the ANXA1-interacting partners were obtained by using the SOPMA tool. The results showed that median percentage of the α-helix was lowest in the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (36.67%) as compared with the common ANXA1- (41.40%) and unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (42.93%) (<a href="#fig0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a><strong>A</strong>). Median percentage of the β-turn was lowest in the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (5.14%) as compared with the common ANXA1- (7.14%) and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (6.07%) (<a href="#fig0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a><strong>B</strong>). Similarly, median percentage of the extended strand was lowest in the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (13.28%) as compared with the common ANXA1- (16.44%) and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (17.38%) (<a href="#fig0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a><strong>D</strong>). The median percentage of the random coil was lowest in the common ANXA1-interacting partners (35.10%) as compared with the unique ApANXA1- (38.18%) and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (39.01%) (<a href="#fig0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a><strong>C</strong>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0030" title="Fig. 6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/b2eec6b3c3b9/gr6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="678" width="776" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0030/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: Analysis of secondary structure of the ANXA1-interacting partners. (A-D):</strong> Box plots of alpha-helices, beta-turns, random coils and extended strands/sheets, respectively.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec0105"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7. Functional enrichment analysis of the ANXA1-interacting partners</h3>
<p id="p0140">GO molecular functions of the ANXA1-interacting partners by using the STRING tool revealed that all sets of these proteins were involved in RNA binding, cadherin binding, and structural constituent of ribosome (<a href="#fig0035" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>). Fifteen GO molecular functions, including small ribosomal subunit rRNA binding, phospholipase A2 inhibitor activity, cell adhesion mediator activity, endopeptidase inhibitor activity, protease binding, unfolded protein binding, MHC class II protein complex binding, enzyme inhibitor activity, ubiquitin protein ligase binding, nucleoside-triphosphatase activity, protein-containing complex binding, enzyme binding, identical protein binding, cell adhesion molecule binding, and structural molecule activity, were observed in the common ANXA1-interacting partners (<a href="#fig0035" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>). Three GO molecular functions, including cadherin binding involved in cell-cell adhesion, calcium-dependent protein binding, and S100 protein binding, were exclusively observed in the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners. However, no unique molecular function was observed for the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners. Most of the molecular functions enriched in the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners overlapped with those enriched in the common ANXA1-interacting partners (<a href="#fig0035" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0035" title="Fig. 7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/f96ca12857cf/gr7.jpg" loading="lazy" height="642" width="776" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0035/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: GO molecular functions of the ANXA1-interacting partners.</strong> † = GO molecular functions enriched in all three sets of the interacting partners, § = GO molecular functions enriched only in the common ANXA1-interacting partners, and * = GO molecular functions enriched only in the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners. Note that there was no unique molecular function observed for the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0145">GO biological process, reactome pathway and KEGG pathway analyses by using Cytoscape-based ClueGO tool revealed that the common ANXA1-interacting partners were mainly involved in metabolic processes and plasminogen activation (<a href="#fig0040" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a><strong>A</strong>), whereas the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners were involved mainly in protein ubiquitination (<a href="#fig0040" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a><strong>B</strong>) and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners were involved mainly in protein localization to mitochondria (<a href="#fig0040" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a><strong>C</strong>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0040" title="Fig. 8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10407547_gr8.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d559/10407547/691b19c745cc/gr8.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1407" width="665" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0040/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>: GO biological processes, reactome pathways and KEGG pathways of the ANXA1-interacting partners. (A):</strong> Common ANXA1-interacting partners. <strong>(B):</strong> Unique ApANXA1-interacting partners. <strong>(C):</strong> Unique CyANXA1-interacting partners.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="sec0110"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p id="p0150">ANXA1 is a multifunctional protein involved in various cellular processes and localized in both cytosolic and membrane compartments <a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">[1]</a>, <a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2">[2]</a>. Usually, proteins work together as a protein complex with their partners. Herein, we characterized the ANXA1-interacting proteins and their roles in both apical membrane and cytosolic compartments of renal epithelial cells under high-calcium condition (to upregulate expression levels of both ApANXA1 and CyANXA1 in renal epithelial cells <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>). Apical membrane and cytosolic compartments were fractionated by the peeling method, which has been established previously <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a>. This method is simple and very effective to isolate apical membrane proteins with high purity as confirmed in previous studies <a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">[30]</a>, <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a> and herein. The method is based on hydrous affinity and/or ionic interaction between a pre-wetted filter paper and apical membrane of the intact polarized cells <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a>. Comparing with other isolation methods, such as precipitation with MgCl<sub>2</sub> or polyethylene glycol followed by sucrose-density gradient centrifugation or differential centrifugation, the peeling method is more effective without significant contaminations from basolateral membrane, cytosolic compartment and intracellular organelles <a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">[36]</a>. Moreover, as the peeling method is free of any chemicals during the isolation processes, the physico-chemical properties of apical membrane proteins should be better preserved as compared with the precipitation-based methods, in which chemicals may introduce modifications to the proteins and interfere with PPI <a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41">[41]</a>, <a href="#bib42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib42">[42]</a>. In addition, the peeling method has been proven to be very effective for many other studies to isolate and investigate the apical membrane proteins in several various aspects <a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34">[34]</a>, <a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">[37]</a>, <a href="#bib43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib43">[43]</a>, <a href="#bib44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib44">[44]</a>, <a href="#bib45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib45">[45]</a>, <a href="#bib46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib46">[46]</a>, <a href="#bib47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib47">[47]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0155">IP-MS/MS analysis of the ANXA1-interacting partners in apical membrane and cytosolic fractions of renal epithelial cells revealed that number of the ANXA1-interating proteins in cytosolic fraction was greater than that in apical membrane fraction. Some of these identified proteins were present in both fractions. However, there were some unique interacting partners that were exclusively identified in each compartment. We therefore divided the ANXA1-interacting partners into three main sets, including the common ANXA1-interacting partners (37 proteins, which were found in both apical membrane and cytosolic fractions), unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (19 proteins, which were found only in apical membrane fraction), and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (32 proteins, which were found only in cytosolic fraction).</p>
<p id="p0160">S100A11 protein was identified as one of the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners and showed direct association with ANXA1 in the PPI network. In concordance, previous studies have reported that calcium facilitates the interaction between ANXA1 and protein S100 family <a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48">[48]</a>, <a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">[49]</a>. A few other studies have reported the role of S100A11 in calcium-dependent binding of ANXA1 to phospholipids <a href="#bib50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib50">[50]</a>, <a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51">[51]</a>. In kidney stone research, S100 proteins have been found in stone matrix <a href="#bib52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib52">[52]</a> with increased levels in urinary exosomes isolated from the stone patients <a href="#bib53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib53">[53]</a>. Herein, GO molecular function enrichment analysis have shown that the unique ApANXA1-binding partners were exclusively involved in calcium-dependent protein binding and S100 protein binding. Moreover, ezrin was identified as another unique ApANXA1-interacting partner, which directly interacted with ANXA1. Ezrin is a membrane-bound protein that belongs to the ERM (ezrin/radixin/moesin) protein family <a href="#bib54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib54">[54]</a>. Ezrin usually serves as a linker between plasma membrane and filamentous actin <a href="#bib54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib54">[54]</a>. Therefore, it plays a major role in regulation of epithelial microvillar structure <a href="#bib55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib55">[55]</a>. Previous studies have suggested that ezrin can bind to several members of S100 proteins <a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56">[56]</a>, <a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57">[57]</a>. S100P proteins interact with ezrin in calcium-dependent manner to regulate actin cytoskeleton-ezrin association <a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57">[57]</a>. The dissociation of actin cytoskeleton from ezrin has been found in the CaOx-treated renal cells <a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19">[19]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0165">According to a previous gel-based proteomics study, there are at least two forms of ANXA1 (at approximately 37 and 100 kDa) detected in renal tubular cells <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>. Additionally, levels of these two ANXA1 forms are increased by high-calcium condition <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>. They have been confirmed as ANXA1 by mass spectrometry and Western blotting <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>. Studies on other annexins have found calcium-dependent trimer and multimer, particularly dimer of the trimer of the membrane-bound ANXA5 and ANXB12 <a href="#bib58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib58">[58]</a>, <a href="#bib59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib59">[59]</a>, <a href="#bib60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib60">[60]</a>, <a href="#bib61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib61">[61]</a>. Moreover, the calcium-dependent heterotetrametric forms of ANXA1 and ANXA2 together with S100A protein have been found at membrane phospholipid layers <a href="#bib62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib62">[62]</a>, <a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63">[63]</a>, <a href="#bib64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib64">[64]</a>, <a href="#bib65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib65">[65]</a>. Interestingly, ANXA1, S100 protein and ezrin have been characterized as members of the CaOx crystal receptors <a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19">[19]</a>, <a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">[28]</a>, <a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">[30]</a>, <a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31">[31]</a>, <a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34">[34]</a>. Therefore, it is highly possible that the high-molecular-weight form of ANXA1 found at apical membrane fraction (or ApANXA1) is the calcium-induced multimeric form of ANXA1 that specifically serves as the CaOx receptor and may function differently from CyANXA1. Moreover, the unique interactions among these three CaOx crystal receptors on apical membrane would enhance CaOx crystal adhesion and retention on apical membrane of renal tubular cells, leading to kidney stone pathogenesis.</p>
<p id="p0170">For biological processes, functional enrichment analysis demonstrated that the common ANXA1-interacting partners were involved in several metabolic processes, such as glycolysis, ATP generation from ADP, and ATP biogenesis. In concordance, a previous study has demonstrated that ANXA1 can bind ATP <a href="#bib66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib66">[66]</a>. Additionally, ANXA1 plays roles in regulation of ATP-induced inflammasome activation <a href="#bib67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib67">[67]</a>, <a href="#bib68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib68">[68]</a>. Multiple lines of additional evidence have also indicated that ANXA1 plays a potential role as an anti-inflammatory protein that is upregulated by several inducers <a href="#bib68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib68">[68]</a>, <a href="#bib69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib69">[69]</a>. Interestingly, the regulation of inflammation by ANXA1 is accompanied with several proteins localized in both cytosolic and membrane compartments <a href="#bib70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib70">[70]</a>.</p>
<p id="p0175">Analyses of physico-chemical properties showed that molecular weights of the ANXA1-interacting partners were not quite different across the three sets. However, the unique ApAXNA1-interacting partners tended to have higher molecular weights as compared with the others. Analysis of the protein pI demonstrated that pI of the common ANXA1-interacting partners was similar to that of the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners, whereas the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners showed higher pI (more basic). Instability index analysis indicated that majority of the common ANXA1- and unique ApANXA1-interacting partners were relatively more stable proteins, whereas the relatively more unstable proteins were predominantly found in the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners. Additionally, aliphatic index analysis demonstrated that the thermostability of the common ANXA1-interacting partners was comparable to that of the unique CyANXA1 interacting partners, but different from the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners, which showed lower aliphatic index. GRAVY score indicating the hydrophilicity/hydrophobicity of proteins demonstrated that almost all the ANXA1-interacting partners were hydrophilic proteins. This is somewhat surprising that almost all the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners were hydrophilic, because it is well known that membrane proteins are likely to be hydrophobic. However, this may be explained that while ApANXA1 localizes at the membrane, its interacting partners are just associated with it and not necessarily to be the membrane proteins themselves (i.e., serve as the membrane-associated proteins).</p>
<p id="p0180">Analysis of secondary structure features in each set revealed that most of the secondary conformations found in almost all the ANXA1-interating partners were α-helices. Previous study has suggested that α-helices are less susceptible to mutations than β-strands <a href="#bib71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib71">[71]</a>. Comparing with other sets, the unique CyANXA1-interacting partners had lowest percentage of α-helices but highest percentage of random coils. By contrast, the unique ApANXA1-interacting partners had lowest percentages of β-turns and extended strands.</p>
<p id="p0185">In conclusion, we report herein identities and characterizations of the interacting partners of ANXA1 in both apical membrane and cytosolic compartments of renal epithelial cells under high-calcium condition. PPI network analysis, analyses of physico-chemical properties, secondary structure prediction, and functional enrichment analysis of these identified proteins have demonstrated that each set of them, including the common ANXA1-interacting partners (found in both cellular compartments), unique ApANXA1-interacting partners (exclusively found in apical membrane compartment), and unique CyANXA1-interacting partners (exclusively found in cytosolic compartment), share some of these characteristics and properties. However, they also have some differences in these characteristics and properties. The knowledge from this study may lead to better understanding of the ApANXA1 and CyAXNA1 biochemistry and functions as well as the pathophysiology of CaOx kidney stone formation induced by high-calcium condition.</p></section><section id="sec0115"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">CRediT authorship contribution statement</h2>
<p id="p0190">PP and VT designed research; PP and WB performed experiments; PP, WB and VT analyzed data; PP and VT wrote the manuscript; All authors reviewed and approved the manuscript.</p></section><section id="sec24"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Declaration of Competing Interest</h2>
